People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... well, I came in because I just can’t seem to shake this feeling. It’s this incredibly heavy, just... completely hopeless feeling. I don't know if it makes sense, but I’m a civil engineer, so I tend to look at things in terms of structures and foundations, and... well, after my divorce, I really thought I had laid a solid new foundation for myself. I spent all this time and energy trying to rebuild my life, meticulously putting every piece back together, making sure my career was on track, getting my own place, taking on these big infrastructure projects at work. 

But lately, it just feels like that entire structure has completely collapsed. Like every single thing I built after the divorce just suffered this massive, catastrophic structural failure, and I’m just... I'm just sitting under it. Under all the rubble. It’s this physical weight, almost like concrete on my chest, and I just sit there thinking about how I'm supposed to dig myself out when I can't even see the light anymore. People keep telling me it's going to get better, or that I just need to give it time, but they don't understand what it's like to be trapped under all that weight, knowing that everything you worked so hard to put up is just gone. I just sit there under it all, every single day, and I don't know how to fix it.

Well, yes, I suppose... I mean, it all ties back to the divorce, obviously, but specifically, it was the financial fallout that really set this off. You see, when we finalized everything, I really thought the worst of the negotiations and the splitting of assets was behind me. I had budgeted everything out—spreadsheets, projections, exactly how I was going to manage the new place, the remaining legal fees, my living expenses. I had a solid plan.

I was looking at these numbers, these massive, unexpected deficits, and that’s exactly when this heavy, suffocating feeling started.

I... I don't know. I really don't. Because, see, I've thought about that. I... well, yes. Yes, I have. And it's hard to admit that because, as an engineer, I'm supposed to be the one who solves problems, right? I fix things. I design things to withstand pressure. But the pressure has just been so constant, so heavy. I've spent the last few weeks just lying there in bed, staring at the ceiling, feeling that concrete on my chest, and I started thinking... I started thinking that it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up. If I could just close my eyes and the structural failure wouldn't matter anymore because I wouldn't be here to feel the weight of the rubble. 

It started as just a passing thought, you know? Just a quiet little idea that maybe the easiest way to fix the deficit, to fix the collapse, was just to remove myself from the equation entirely. But then... then it started getting louder. The thoughts started getting so intense, so specific, and they were happening all the time. I was sitting at my desk at work, looking at these blueprints for a new overpass, and all I could think about was how much easier it would be to just... stop. To just not exist anymore. 

It got to the point where I actually scared myself. I realized I wasn't just thinking about it in the abstract anymore; the hopelessness was so absolute that not waking up felt like the only logical solution left on the table. That terrified me. It really did. Because I know that's not right, I know that's a catastrophic failure of my own internal systems. So, I finally broke down and called the employee assistance program at work. I just dialed the number because I knew if I didn't tell someone how close I was to just letting the whole thing cave in on me, I might actually do it. So... yes. I've had those thoughts. A lot.

You see, when we finalized everything, I really thought the worst of the negotiations and the splitting of assets was behind me. I had budgeted everything out—spreadsheets, projections, exactly how I was going to manage the new place, the remaining legal fees, my living expenses. I had a solid plan. \n\nBut then, about a month ago... God, has it really only been a month? It feels like it’s been years. But yes, about four weeks ago, this absolute financial mess just landed right in my lap. There were these lingering tax liabilities and hidden debts from his side that somehow got attached to my accounts, and suddenly my entire financial model was just... shattered. I was looking at these numbers, these massive, unexpected deficits, and that’s exactly when this heavy, suffocating feeling started. \n\nIt’s been hanging over me every single day for the past month. I wake up, and the reality of that financial ruin is just sitting there on my chest. I tried to run the numbers again, you know, trying to engineer a way out, maybe restructure things or figure out a new baseline, but every time I look at the bank statements, my brain just completely shuts down. For an entire month now, I haven't been able to see past this gaping hole in my foundation. And he just gets to walk away, while I'm stuck here for the last thirty days trying to figure out how I'm going to survive when the math literally doesn't add up anymore. I just... I don't know how to fix a deficit this big, and the weight of it has just been crushing me non-stop since it all came to light.\n\nDoctor: If this financial problem were suddenly resolved, do you think the hopeless feeling would mostly lift?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. I really don't. Because, see, I've thought about that. I've laid awake at night—well, when I'm just lying there staring at the ceiling—thinking, if I could just get a loan, or if the lawyers could somehow magically untangle this and put the debt back on him where it belongs... if the spreadsheet suddenly balanced, would I be okay? \n\nAnd honestly? I don't think I would be. Because it's not just the math anymore. It's... it's like I was telling you when I first sat down. I came in today because I just can't shake this incredibly heavy, hopeless feeling. It's not just a reaction to a bad bank statement. It feels like everything—literally everything—I built after the divorce has just completely collapsed. It's a total structural failure of my life. \n\nEven if you handed me a check right now to cover every penny of that debt, I’d still be sitting under all this rubble. The money was just the... the stressor that caused the fracture, but the collapse has already happened. The concrete is already sitting on my chest. I can't just stand up and dust myself off because the foundation is gone. The hopelessness... it’s seeped into the ground under me. So, no, I don't think fixing the money would fix this. I'm just trapped under it, and I can't see a way out, no matter what the numbers say.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living or that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I... well, yes. Yes, I have. And it's hard to admit that because, as an engineer, I'm supposed to be the one who solves problems, right? I fix things. I design things to withstand pressure. But the pressure has just been so constant, so heavy. I've spent the last few weeks just lying there in bed, staring at the ceiling, feeling that concrete on my chest, and I started thinking... I started thinking that it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up. If I could just close my eyes and the structural failure wouldn't matter anymore because I wouldn't be here to feel the weight of the rubble. \n\nIt started as just a passing thought, you know? Just a quiet little idea that maybe the easiest way to fix the deficit, to fix the collapse, was just to remove myself from the equation entirely. But then... then it started getting louder. The thoughts started getting so intense, so specific, and they were happening all the time. I was sitting at my desk at work, looking at these blueprints for a new overpass, and all I could think about was how much easier it would be to just... stop. To just not exist anymore. \n\nIt got to the point where I actually scared myself. It really did. Because I know that's not right, I know that's a catastrophic failure of my own internal systems. So, I finally broke down and called the employee assistance program at work. I just dialed the number because I knew if I didn't tell someone how close I was to just letting the whole thing cave in on me, I might actually do it. So... yes. I've had those thoughts. A lot.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don't think so. I mean, I've had periods of stress, obviously. College was incredibly stressful, especially the upper-level engineering courses—fluid mechanics, structural analysis, those were brutal semesters. There were definitely times when I felt overwhelmed, maybe even a little down, pulling all-nighters and drinking way too much coffee, just trying to keep my head above water. But that was different. That was situational stress, and it always had an end date. Once finals were over, or once a major project was submitted, the pressure lifted, and I was back to my normal baseline. 

And then there was the divorce itself, of course. That was a terrible time. The initial shock, the arguments, the realization that the life I had planned out was completely changing... it was incredibly sad. I cried a lot, and there were days when I didn't want to get out of bed, but it never felt like this. It never felt like this absolute, crushing weight that I couldn't escape from. Even during the worst parts of the separation, I still had hope. I still believed that once the paperwork was signed and we went our separate ways, I could rebuild. I could draft a new plan and start over. 

But this... what I've been feeling for the past month... this is entirely new. I've never experienced this level of darkness before. It's not just feeling sad or stressed; it's a complete absence of light. It's like the fundamental laws of physics in my brain have just stopped working, and everything is collapsing inward. I've never had a period in my life where I felt this hopeless, this completely trapped under the rubble, for weeks on end. This is the first time I've ever felt like my internal structure has just completely failed. So, no, before this past month, I've never had anything even remotely close to this.

I realized I wasn't just thinking about it in the abstract anymore; the hopelessness was so absolute that not waking up felt like the only logical solution left on the table. That terrified me. It really did. Because I know that's not right, I know that's a catastrophic failure of my own internal systems. So, I finally broke down and called the employee assistance program at work. I just dialed the number because I knew if I didn't tell someone how close I was to just letting the whole thing cave in on me, I might actually do it. So... yes. I've had those thoughts. A lot.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed or hopeless like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I've had periods of stress, obviously. College was incredibly stressful, especially the upper-level engineering courses—fluid mechanics, structural analysis, those were brutal semesters. There were definitely times when I felt overwhelmed, maybe even a little down, pulling all-nighters and drinking way too much coffee, just trying to keep my head above water. But that was different. That was situational stress, and it always had an end date. Once finals were over, or once a major project was submitted, the pressure lifted, and I was back to my normal baseline. \n\nAnd then there was the divorce itself, of course. That was a terrible time. The initial shock, the arguments, the realization that the life I had planned out was completely changing... it was incredibly sad. I cried a lot, and there were days when I didn't want to get out of bed, but it never felt like this. It never felt like this absolute, crushing weight that I couldn't escape from. Even during the worst parts of the separation, I still had hope. I still believed that once the paperwork was signed and we went our separate ways, I could rebuild. I could draft a new plan and start over. \n\nBut this... what I've been feeling for the past month... this is entirely new. I've never experienced this level of darkness before. It's not just feeling sad or stressed; it's a complete absence of light. It's like the fundamental laws of physics in my brain have just stopped working, and everything is collapsing inward. I've never had a period in my life where I felt this hopeless, this completely trapped under the rubble, for weeks on end. This is the first time I've ever felt like my internal structure has just completely failed. So, no, before this past month, I've never had anything even remotely close to this.\n\n[Last Question]\nOver the past month, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... well, I haven't really noticed a specific loss of interest in things, per se. I mean, I guess it's hard to say. Normally, I really love hiking. I'm a big outdoors person. I usually try to get out to the state park at least every other weekend, you know, just to clear my head and get some fresh air. It's always been my way to reset. I have this whole route I take, up the ridge trail, where you can see the entire valley. It's beautiful. And I usually really enjoy reading, too—historical biographies, mostly. I have a whole stack of them on my nightstand right now. 

But lately, with everything that's been going on, with this whole structural collapse I was talking about... I just haven't been doing those things. It's not necessarily that I don't *want* to do them, or that I think I wouldn't enjoy them if I did... it's just that the concrete is so heavy. I'm so exhausted from just trying to breathe under all this rubble that the idea of putting on hiking boots and driving out to the trail just feels impossible. It's like the activation energy required to do anything other than lie in bed staring at the ceiling is just too high. 

So, I haven't been hiking, and I haven't touched those books on my nightstand. I'll look at them, and I'll think, "I should read a chapter," but then I just... don't. I just lie there. But I don't know if that means I've lost *interest* in them. If you asked me right now if I still like hiking, I'd say yes. I still think it's a great activity. I still appreciate the concept of it. It's just that right now, in this current state of total system failure, I can't seem to muster the physical or mental capacity to actually engage with it. Does that make sense? It's more of an inability to act than a lack of desire, I think. Or maybe they're the same thing at this point. I really don't know.

I... you know, it's actually strange, but no. I haven't really been crying. You'd think I would be, right? With all this heaviness and this absolute collapse I've been talking about, you would expect the natural output to be tears. But it's not. 

I mean, earlier I mentioned crying during the divorce, and that was a very... a very wet, messy kind of grief. My tear ducts were just working overtime back then. But this past month? It's like the water main has just been shut off entirely. I feel the pressure, the incredible structural load on my chest, but there's no release valve. I just lie there completely dry-eyed. 

I think... I think I'm almost too exhausted to cry, if that makes any sense. Because crying actually takes energy, doesn't it? It takes a certain amount of muscular effort and emotional bandwidth to actually produce tears and sob, and I just don't have that in my reserves right now. It's just this dry, heavy, crushing sensation. Sometimes I actually wish I could cry, thinking maybe it would relieve some of the tension in the concrete, maybe wash some of the rubble away, but it just doesn't happen. I'll stare at the ceiling for hours, feeling completely devastated, feeling like everything is over, but my face is completely dry. It's bizarre, honestly, because logically, looking at the data of how I'm feeling, the output should definitely be tears. But the system is just... it's completely locked up. So, no, the tears just aren't there.

I've laid awake at night—well, when I'm just lying there staring at the ceiling—thinking, if I could just get a loan, or if the lawyers could somehow magically untangle this and put the debt back on him where it belongs... if the spreadsheet suddenly balanced, would I be okay? \n\nAnd honestly? I don't think I would be. Because it's not just the math anymore. It's... it's like I was telling you when I first sat down. I came in today because I just can't shake this incredibly heavy, hopeless feeling. It's not just a reaction to a bad bank statement. It feels like everything—literally everything—I built after the divorce has just completely collapsed. It's a total structural failure of my life. \n\nEven if you handed me a check right now to cover every penny of that debt, I’d still be sitting under all this rubble. The money was just the... the stressor that caused the fracture, but the collapse has already happened. The concrete is already sitting on my chest. I can't just stand up and dust myself off because the foundation is gone. The hopelessness... it’s seeped into the ground under me. So, no, I don't think fixing the money would fix this. I'm just trapped under it, and I can't see a way out, no matter what the numbers say.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living or that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I... well, yes. Yes, I have. And it's hard to admit that because, as an engineer, I'm supposed to be the one who solves problems, right? I fix things. I design things to withstand pressure. But the pressure has just been so constant, so heavy. I've spent the last few weeks just lying there in bed, staring at the ceiling, feeling that concrete on my chest, and I started thinking... I started thinking that it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up. If I could just close my eyes and the structural failure wouldn't matter anymore because I wouldn't be here to feel the weight of the rubble. \n\nIt started as just a passing thought, you know? Just a quiet little idea that maybe the easiest way to fix the deficit, to fix the collapse, was just to remove myself from the equation entirely. But then... then it started getting louder. The thoughts started getting so intense, so specific, and they were happening all the time. I was sitting at my desk at work, looking at these blueprints for a new overpass, and all I could think about was how much easier it would be to just... stop. To just not exist anymore. \n\nIt got to the point where I actually scared myself. I realized I wasn't just thinking about it in the abstract anymore; the hopelessness was so absolute that not waking up felt like the only logical solution left on the table. That terrified me. It really did. Because I know that's not right, I know that's a catastrophic failure of my own internal systems. So, I finally broke down and called the employee assistance program at work. I just dialed the number because I knew if I didn't tell someone how close I was to just letting the whole thing cave in on me, I might actually do it. So... yes. I've had those thoughts. A lot.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed or hopeless like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I've had periods of stress, obviously. College was incredibly stressful, especially the upper-level engineering courses—fluid mechanics, structural analysis, those were brutal semesters. There were definitely times when I felt overwhelmed, maybe even a little down, pulling all-nighters and drinking way too much coffee, just trying to keep my head above water. But that was different. That was situational stress, and it always had an end date. Once finals were over, or once a major project was submitted, the pressure lifted, and I was back to my normal baseline. \n\nAnd then there was the divorce itself, of course. That was a terrible time. The initial shock, the arguments, the realization that the life I had planned out was completely changing... it was incredibly sad. I cried a lot, and there were days when I didn't want to get out of bed, but it never felt like this. It never felt like this absolute, crushing weight that I couldn't escape from. Even during the worst parts of the separation, I still had hope. I still believed that once the paperwork was signed and we went our separate ways, I could rebuild. I could draft a new plan and start over. \n\nBut this... what I've been feeling for the past month... this is entirely new. I've never experienced this level of darkness before. It's not just feeling sad or stressed; it's a complete absence of light. It's like the fundamental laws of physics in my brain have just stopped working, and everything is collapsing inward. I've never had a period in my life where I felt this hopeless, this completely trapped under the rubble, for weeks on end. This is the first time I've ever felt like my internal structure has just completely failed. So, no, before this past month, I've never had anything even remotely close to this.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I... well, I haven't really noticed a specific loss of interest in things, per se. I mean, I guess it's hard to say. Normally, I really love hiking. I'm a big outdoors person. I usually try to get out to the state park at least every other weekend, you know, just to clear my head and get some fresh air. It's always been my way to reset. I have this whole route I take, up the ridge trail, where you can see the entire valley. It's beautiful. And I usually really enjoy reading, too—historical biographies, mostly. I have a whole stack of them on my nightstand right now. \n\nBut lately, with everything that's been going on, with this whole structural collapse I was talking about... I just haven't been doing those things. It's not necessarily that I don't *want* to do them, or that I think I wouldn't enjoy them if I did... it's just that the concrete is so heavy. I'm so exhausted from just trying to breathe under all this rubble that the idea of putting on hiking boots and driving out to the trail just feels impossible. It's like the activation energy required to do anything other than lie in bed staring at the ceiling is just too high. \n\nSo, I haven't been hiking, and I haven't touched those books on my nightstand. I'll look at them, and I'll think, \"I should read a chapter,\" but then I just... don't. I just lie there. But I don't know if that means I've lost *interest* in them. If you asked me right now if I still like hiking, I'd say yes. I still think it's a great activity. I still appreciate the concept of it. It's just that right now, in this current state of total system failure, I can't seem to muster the physical or mental capacity to actually engage with it. Does that make sense? It's more of an inability to act than a lack of desire, I think. Or maybe they're the same thing at this point. I really don't know.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been crying a lot?\n\nPatient: I... you know, it's actually strange, but no. I haven't really been crying. You'd think I would be, right? With all this heaviness and this absolute collapse I've been talking about, you would expect the natural output to be tears. But this past month? It's like the water main has just been shut off entirely. I feel the pressure, the incredible structural load on my chest, but there's no release valve. I just lie there completely dry-eyed. \n\nI think... I think I'm almost too exhausted to cry, if that makes any sense. Because crying actually takes energy, doesn't it? It takes a certain amount of muscular effort and emotional bandwidth to actually produce tears and sob, and I just don't have that in my reserves right now. It's just this dry, heavy, crushing sensation. Sometimes I actually wish I could cry, thinking maybe it would relieve some of the tension in the concrete, maybe wash some of the rubble away, but it just doesn't happen. I'll stare at the ceiling for hours, feeling completely devastated, feeling like everything is over, but my face is completely dry. It's bizarre, honestly, because logically, looking at the data of how I'm feeling, the output should definitely be tears. But the system is just... it's completely locked up. So, no, the tears just aren't there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sleep? God, my sleep is just... it's a complete disaster. It's completely non-functional. You know how a bridge is supposed to have a period of low stress, low traffic, to just exist without bearing maximum load? Sleep is supposed to be that for the brain, right? But I'm not getting that. At all. 

Lately, it's like my mind is a turbine that someone left running on high, but there's no water flowing through it, just grinding metal. I'll get into bed, usually around 10:30, because that's always been my schedule, right? I'm very regimented usually. But I'll just lie there. I'll lie awake staring at the ceiling, and the loop starts. It's this endless, agonizing loop where I'm replaying everything. I'll think about the bills—the mortgage on the house that I'm now paying alone, the utilities, the credit card I had to put some of the legal fees on. I start doing the math in my head, over and over, projecting out months in advance, and the numbers never work out. 

And if it's not the bills, it's work. I'll obsess over a site plan I reviewed three weeks ago. I'll suddenly convince myself I missed a crucial load-bearing calculation on that commercial project downtown. I'll try to visually reconstruct the blueprints in my head in the dark, terrified that I've approved something that's going to collapse, which is ironic given how I feel internally. This will go on for hours. I'll look at the clock and it's 1:00 AM, then 2:30 AM, then 3:45 AM. 

And then, if I do finally manage to drift off, it's not real sleep. It's this thin, fragile layer of unconsciousness that shatters at the slightest thing. I'll wake up before dawn, like 4:30 or 5:00 in the morning, and my heart is just pounding. The anxiety is instantly there, sitting on my chest like a concrete block. And I feel completely unrefreshed. I feel like I never actually slept at all, like I just closed my eyes and continued worrying on a subconscious level. I drag myself out of bed feeling heavier and more exhausted than when I got in it the night before. It's just a completely unsustainable cycle of fatigue.

I tried to run the numbers again, you know, trying to engineer a way out, maybe restructure things or figure out a new baseline, but every time I look at the bank statements, my brain just completely shuts down. For an entire month now, I haven't been able to see past this gaping hole in my foundation. And he just gets to walk away, while I'm stuck here for the last thirty days trying to figure out how I'm going to survive when the math literally doesn't add up anymore. I just... I don't know how to fix a deficit this big, and the weight of it has just been crushing me non-stop since it all came to light.\n\nDoctor: If this financial problem were suddenly resolved, do you think the hopeless feeling would mostly lift?\n\nPatient: I... I don't know. I really don't. Because, see, I've thought about that. I've laid awake at night—well, when I'm just lying there staring at the ceiling—thinking, if I could just get a loan, or if the lawyers could somehow magically untangle this and put the debt back on him where it belongs... if the spreadsheet suddenly balanced, would I be okay? \n\nAnd honestly? I don't think I would be. Because it's not just the math anymore. It's... it's like I was telling you when I first sat down. I came in today because I just can't shake this incredibly heavy, hopeless feeling. It's not just a reaction to a bad bank statement. It feels like everything—literally everything—I built after the divorce has just completely collapsed. It's a total structural failure of my life. \n\nEven if you handed me a check right now to cover every penny of that debt, I’d still be sitting under all this rubble. The money was just the... the stressor that caused the fracture, but the collapse has already happened. The concrete is already sitting on my chest. I can't just stand up and dust myself off because the foundation is gone. The hopelessness... it’s seeped into the ground under me. So, no, I don't think fixing the money would fix this. I'm just trapped under it, and I can't see a way out, no matter what the numbers say.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living or that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I... well, yes. Yes, I have. And it's hard to admit that because, as an engineer, I'm supposed to be the one who solves problems, right? I fix things. I design things to withstand pressure. But the pressure has just been so constant, so heavy. I've spent the last few weeks just lying there in bed, staring at the ceiling, feeling that concrete on my chest, and I started thinking... I started thinking that it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up. If I could just close my eyes and the structural failure wouldn't matter anymore because I wouldn't be here to feel the weight of the rubble. \n\nIt started as just a passing thought, you know? Just a quiet little idea that maybe the easiest way to fix the deficit, to fix the collapse, was just to remove myself from the equation entirely. But then... then it started getting louder. The thoughts started getting so intense, so specific, and they were happening all the time. I was sitting at my desk at work, looking at these blueprints for a new overpass, and all I could think about was how much easier it would be to just... stop. To just not exist anymore. \n\nIt got to the point where I actually scared myself. I realized I wasn't just thinking about it in the abstract anymore; the hopelessness was so absolute that not waking up felt like the only logical solution left on the table. That terrified me. It really did. Because I know that's not right, I know that's a catastrophic failure of my own internal systems. So, I finally broke down and called the employee assistance program at work. I just dialed the number because I knew if I didn't tell someone how close I was to just letting the whole thing cave in on me, I might actually do it. So... yes. I've had those thoughts. A lot.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed or hopeless like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I've had periods of stress, obviously. College was incredibly stressful, especially the upper-level engineering courses—fluid mechanics, structural analysis, those were brutal semesters. There were definitely times when I felt overwhelmed, maybe even a little down, pulling all-nighters and drinking way too much coffee, just trying to keep my head above water. But that was different. That was situational stress, and it always had an end date. Once finals were over, or once a major project was submitted, the pressure lifted, and I was back to my normal baseline. \n\nAnd then there was the divorce itself, of course. That was a terrible time. The initial shock, the arguments, the realization that the life I had planned out was completely changing... it was incredibly sad. I cried a lot, and there were days when I didn't want to get out of bed, but it never felt like this. It never felt like this absolute, crushing weight that I couldn't escape from. Even during the worst parts of the separation, I still had hope. I still believed that once the paperwork was signed and we went our separate ways, I could rebuild. I could draft a new plan and start over. \n\nBut this... what I've been feeling for the past month... this is entirely new. I've never experienced this level of darkness before. It's not just feeling sad or stressed; it's a complete absence of light. It's like the fundamental laws of physics in my brain have just stopped working, and everything is collapsing inward. I've never had a period in my life where I felt this hopeless, this completely trapped under the rubble, for weeks on end. This is the first time I've ever felt like my internal structure has just completely failed. So, no, before this past month, I've never had anything even remotely close to this.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I... well, I haven't really noticed a specific loss of interest in things, per se. I mean, I guess it's hard to say. Normally, I really love hiking. I'm a big outdoors person. I usually try to get out to the state park at least every other weekend, you know, just to clear my head and get some fresh air. It's always been my way to reset. I have this whole route I take, up the ridge trail, where you can see the entire valley. It's beautiful. And I usually really enjoy reading, too—historical biographies, mostly. I have a whole stack of them on my nightstand right now. \n\nBut lately, with everything that's been going on, with this whole structural collapse I was talking about... I just haven't been doing those things. It's not necessarily that I don't *want* to do them, or that I think I wouldn't enjoy them if I did... it's just that the concrete is so heavy. I'm so exhausted from just trying to breathe under all this rubble that the idea of putting on hiking boots and driving out to the trail just feels impossible. It's like the activation energy required to do anything other than lie in bed staring at the ceiling is just too high. \n\nSo, I haven't been hiking, and I haven't touched those books on my nightstand. I'll look at them, and I'll think, \"I should read a chapter,\" but then I just... don't. I just lie there. But I don't know if that means I've lost *interest* in them. If you asked me right now if I still like hiking, I'd say yes. I still think it's a great activity. I still appreciate the concept of it. It's just that right now, in this current state of total system failure, I can't seem to muster the physical or mental capacity to actually engage with it. Does that make sense? It's more of an inability to act than a lack of desire, I think. Or maybe they're the same thing at this point. I really don't know.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been crying a lot?\n\nPatient: I... you know, it's actually strange, but no. I haven't really been crying. You'd think I would be, right? With all this heaviness and this absolute collapse I've been talking about, you would expect the natural output to be tears. But it's not. \n\nI mean, earlier I mentioned crying during the divorce, and that was a very... a very wet, messy kind of grief. My tear ducts were just working overtime back then. But this past month? It's like the water main has just been shut off entirely. I feel the pressure, the incredible structural load on my chest, but there's no release valve. I just lie there completely dry-eyed. \n\nI think... I think I'm almost too exhausted to cry, if that makes any sense. Because crying actually takes energy, doesn't it? It takes a certain amount of muscular effort and emotional bandwidth to actually produce tears and sob, and I just don't have that in my reserves right now. It's just this dry, heavy, crushing sensation. Sometimes I actually wish I could cry, thinking maybe it would relieve some of the tension in the concrete, maybe wash some of the rubble away, but it just doesn't happen. I'll stare at the ceiling for hours, feeling completely devastated, feeling like everything is over, but my face is completely dry. It's bizarre, honestly, because logically, looking at the data of how I'm feeling, the output should definitely be tears. But the system is just... it's completely locked up. So, no, the tears just aren't there.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, how has your sleep been?\n\nPatient: Sleep? God, my sleep is just... it's a complete disaster. It's completely non-functional. You know how a bridge is supposed to have a period of low stress, low traffic, to just exist without bearing maximum load? Sleep is supposed to be that for the brain, right? But I'm not getting that. At all. \n\nLately, it's like my mind is a turbine that someone left running on high, but there's no water flowing through it, just grinding metal. I'll get into bed, usually around 10:30, because that's always been my schedule, right? I'm very regimented usually. But I'll just lie there. I'll lie awake staring at the ceiling, and the loop starts. It's this endless, agonizing loop where I'm replaying everything. I'll think about the bills—the mortgage on the house that I'm now paying alone, the utilities, the credit card I had to put some of the legal fees on. I start doing the math in my head, over and over, projecting out months in advance, and the numbers never work out. \n\nAnd if it's not the bills, it's work. I'll obsess over a site plan I reviewed three weeks ago. I'll suddenly convince myself I missed a crucial load-bearing calculation on that commercial project downtown. I'll try to visually reconstruct the blueprints in my head in the dark, terrified that I've approved something that's going to collapse, which is ironic given how I feel internally. This will go on for hours. I'll look at the clock and it's 1:00 AM, then 2:30 AM, then 3:45 AM. \n\nAnd then, if I do finally manage to drift off, it's not real sleep. It's this thin, fragile layer of unconsciousness that shatters at the slightest thing. I'll wake up before dawn, like 4:30 or 5:00 in the morning, and my heart is just pounding. The anxiety is instantly there, sitting on my chest like a concrete block. And I feel completely unrefreshed. I feel like I never actually slept at all, like I just closed my eyes and continued worrying on a subconscious level. I drag myself out of bed feeling heavier and more exhausted than when I got in it the night before. It's just a completely unsustainable cycle of fatigue.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... I really haven't noticed much of a change, to be honest. I mean, my appetite is just sort of... there. It's the same as it usually is, I suppose. 

You know, it's funny, because you hear about people who get so stressed out or depressed and they just stop eating entirely, right? Like their stomach just shrinks up into a little knot and they can't even look at food without feeling nauseous. Or on the flip side, you hear about people who just eat their feelings—they sit on the couch with a pint of ice cream or a whole pizza and just consume until they're numb. But for me, it hasn't really been either of those extremes. It's just... neutral.

I still eat my normal meals. I'll get up, and even though I'm completely exhausted from that terrible sleep cycle I was just describing, I'll still go to the kitchen and make my usual breakfast. It's always oatmeal with a handful of walnuts and half a banana. I've eaten that same breakfast for, I don't know, six or seven years now. It's a very efficient, structurally sound way to start the day, nutritionally speaking. And I still eat it. I don't necessarily savor it or get any joy out of it right now, but I eat it because it's fuel. It's just a mechanical process. 

And lunch is the same. I usually pack a turkey sandwich on whole wheat or a salad with some grilled chicken. Even with everything feeling like it's crumbling around me, I still go through the motions of putting the food in my mouth and chewing and swallowing. Dinner is maybe a little harder to muster the energy to actually cook—sometimes I'll just heat up a can of soup instead of making a full meal—but I'm still eating a normal amount of food. My stomach still growls around noon, and I still respond to that biological cue. 

So, no, the appetite itself hasn't really changed. The *experience* of eating is definitely completely devoid of pleasure, much like everything else right now, but the actual volume of food and the desire to consume it to keep the machine running... that's all stayed pretty consistent. I haven't lost weight or gained weight or anything like that. It's just one of the few systems that seems to still be operating on its baseline parameters, even if the rest of the structure is failing.

It feels like everything—literally everything—I built after the divorce has just completely collapsed. It's a total structural failure of my life. \n\nEven if you handed me a check right now to cover every penny of that debt, I’d still be sitting under all this rubble. The money was just the... the stressor that caused the fracture, but the collapse has already happened. The concrete is already sitting on my chest. I can't just stand up and dust myself off because the foundation is gone. The hopelessness... it’s seeped into the ground under me. So, no, I don't think fixing the money would fix this. I'm just trapped under it, and I can't see a way out, no matter what the numbers say.\n\nDoctor: Have you had thoughts that life isn’t worth living or that you would be better off dead?\n\nPatient: I... well, yes. Yes, I have. And it's hard to admit that because, as an engineer, I'm supposed to be the one who solves problems, right? I fix things. I design things to withstand pressure. But the pressure has just been so constant, so heavy. I've spent the last few weeks just lying there in bed, staring at the ceiling, feeling that concrete on my chest, and I started thinking... I started thinking that it would just be so much easier if I didn't wake up. If I could just close my eyes and the structural failure wouldn't matter anymore because I wouldn't be here to feel the weight of the rubble. \n\nIt started as just a passing thought, you know? Just a quiet little idea that maybe the easiest way to fix the deficit, to fix the collapse, was just to remove myself from the equation entirely. But then... then it started getting louder. The thoughts started getting so intense, so specific, and they were happening all the time. I was sitting at my desk at work, looking at these blueprints for a new overpass, and all I could think about was how much easier it would be to just... stop. To just not exist anymore. \n\nIt got to the point where I actually scared myself. I realized I wasn't just thinking about it in the abstract anymore; the hopelessness was so absolute that not waking up felt like the only logical solution left on the table. That terrified me. It really did. Because I know that's not right, I know that's a catastrophic failure of my own internal systems. So, I finally broke down and called the employee assistance program at work. I just dialed the number because I knew if I didn't tell someone how close I was to just letting the whole thing cave in on me, I might actually do it. So... yes. I've had those thoughts. A lot.\n\nDoctor: Before this past month, have you ever had another period in your life when you felt depressed or hopeless like this for at least two weeks?\n\nPatient: No, I don't think so. I mean, I've had periods of stress, obviously. College was incredibly stressful, especially the upper-level engineering courses—fluid mechanics, structural analysis, those were brutal semesters. There were definitely times when I felt overwhelmed, maybe even a little down, pulling all-nighters and drinking way too much coffee, just trying to keep my head above water. But that was different. That was situational stress, and it always had an end date. Once finals were over, or once a major project was submitted, the pressure lifted, and I was back to my normal baseline. \n\nAnd then there was the divorce itself, of course. That was a terrible time. The initial shock, the arguments, the realization that the life I had planned out was completely changing... it was incredibly sad. I cried a lot, and there were days when I didn't want to get out of bed, but it never felt like this. It never felt like this absolute, crushing weight that I couldn't escape from. Even during the worst parts of the separation, I still had hope. I still believed that once the paperwork was signed and we went our separate ways, I could rebuild. I could draft a new plan and start over. \n\nBut this... what I've been feeling for the past month... this is entirely new. I've never experienced this level of darkness before. It's not just feeling sad or stressed; it's a complete absence of light. It's like the fundamental laws of physics in my brain have just stopped working, and everything is collapsing inward. I've never had a period in my life where I felt this hopeless, this completely trapped under the rubble, for weeks on end. This is the first time I've ever felt like my internal structure has just completely failed. So, no, before this past month, I've never had anything even remotely close to this.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I... well, I haven't really noticed a specific loss of interest in things, per se. I mean, I guess it's hard to say. Normally, I really love hiking. I'm a big outdoors person. I usually try to get out to the state park at least every other weekend, you know, just to clear my head and get some fresh air. It's always been my way to reset. I have this whole route I take, up the ridge trail, where you can see the entire valley. It's beautiful. And I usually really enjoy reading, too—historical biographies, mostly. I have a whole stack of them on my nightstand right now. \n\nBut lately, with everything that's been going on, with this whole structural collapse I was talking about... I just haven't been doing those things. It's not necessarily that I don't *want* to do them, or that I think I wouldn't enjoy them if I did... it's just that the concrete is so heavy. I'm so exhausted from just trying to breathe under all this rubble that the idea of putting on hiking boots and driving out to the trail just feels impossible. It's like the activation energy required to do anything other than lie in bed staring at the ceiling is just too high. \n\nSo, I haven't been hiking, and I haven't touched those books on my nightstand. I'll look at them, and I'll think, \"I should read a chapter,\" but then I just... don't. I just lie there. But I don't know if that means I've lost *interest* in them. If you asked me right now if I still like hiking, I'd say yes. I still think it's a great activity. I still appreciate the concept of it. It's just that right now, in this current state of total system failure, I can't seem to muster the physical or mental capacity to actually engage with it. Does that make sense? It's more of an inability to act than a lack of desire, I think. Or maybe they're the same thing at this point. I really don't know.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been crying a lot?\n\nPatient: I... you know, it's actually strange, but no. I haven't really been crying. You'd think I would be, right? With all this heaviness and this absolute collapse I've been talking about, you would expect the natural output to be tears. But it's not. \n\nI mean, earlier I mentioned crying during the divorce, and that was a very... a very wet, messy kind of grief. My tear ducts were just working overtime back then. But this past month? It's like the water main has just been shut off entirely. I feel the pressure, the incredible structural load on my chest, but there's no release valve. I just lie there completely dry-eyed. \n\nI think... I think I'm almost too exhausted to cry, if that makes any sense. Because crying actually takes energy, doesn't it? It takes a certain amount of muscular effort and emotional bandwidth to actually produce tears and sob, and I just don't have that in my reserves right now. It's just this dry, heavy, crushing sensation. Sometimes I actually wish I could cry, thinking maybe it would relieve some of the tension in the concrete, maybe wash some of the rubble away, but it just doesn't happen. I'll stare at the ceiling for hours, feeling completely devastated, feeling like everything is over, but my face is completely dry. It's bizarre, honestly, because logically, looking at the data of how I'm feeling, the output should definitely be tears. But the system is just... it's completely locked up. So, no, the tears just aren't there.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, how has your sleep been?\n\nPatient: Sleep? God, my sleep is just... it's a complete disaster. It's completely non-functional. At all. \n\nLately, it's like my mind is a turbine that someone left running on high, but there's no water flowing through it, just grinding metal. I'll get into bed, usually around 10:30, because that's always been my schedule, right? I'm very regimented usually. But I'll just lie there. I'll lie awake staring at the ceiling, and the loop starts. It's this endless, agonizing loop where I'm replaying everything. I'll think about the bills—the mortgage on the house that I'm now paying alone, the utilities, the credit card I had to put some of the legal fees on. I start doing the math in my head, over and over, projecting out months in advance, and the numbers never work out. \n\nAnd if it's not the bills, it's work. I'll obsess over a site plan I reviewed three weeks ago. I'll suddenly convince myself I missed a crucial load-bearing calculation on that commercial project downtown. I'll try to visually reconstruct the blueprints in my head in the dark, terrified that I've approved something that's going to collapse, which is ironic given how I feel internally. This will go on for hours. I'll look at the clock and it's 1:00 AM, then 2:30 AM, then 3:45 AM. \n\nAnd then, if I do finally manage to drift off, it's not real sleep. It's this thin, fragile layer of unconsciousness that shatters at the slightest thing. I'll wake up before dawn, like 4:30 or 5:00 in the morning, and my heart is just pounding. The anxiety is instantly there, sitting on my chest like a concrete block. And I feel completely unrefreshed. I feel like I never actually slept at all, like I just closed my eyes and continued worrying on a subconscious level. I drag myself out of bed feeling heavier and more exhausted than when I got in it the night before. It's just a completely unsustainable cycle of fatigue.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, has your appetite changed?\n\nPatient: I... I really haven't noticed much of a change, to be honest. I'll look at them, and I'll think, \"I should read a chapter,\" but then I just... don't. I just lie there. But I don't know if that means I've lost *interest* in them. If you asked me right now if I still like hiking, I'd say yes. I still think it's a great activity. I still appreciate the concept of it. It's just that right now, in this current state of total system failure, I can't seem to muster the physical or mental capacity to actually engage with it. Does that make sense? It's more of an inability to act than a lack of desire, I think. Or maybe they're the same thing at this point. I really don't know.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, have you been crying a lot?\n\nPatient: I... you know, it's actually strange, but no. I haven't really been crying. You'd think I would be, right? With all this heaviness and this absolute collapse I've been talking about, you would expect the natural output to be tears. But it's not. \n\nI mean, earlier I mentioned crying during the divorce, and that was a very... a very wet, messy kind of grief. My tear ducts were just working overtime back then. But this past month? It's like the water main has just been shut off entirely. I feel the pressure, the incredible structural load on my chest, but there's no release valve. I just lie there completely dry-eyed. \n\nI think... I think I'm almost too exhausted to cry, if that makes any sense. Because crying actually takes energy, doesn't it? It takes a certain amount of muscular effort and emotional bandwidth to actually produce tears and sob, and I just don't have that in my reserves right now. It's just this dry, heavy, crushing sensation. Sometimes I actually wish I could cry, thinking maybe it would relieve some of the tension in the concrete, maybe wash some of the rubble away, but it just doesn't happen. I'll stare at the ceiling for hours, feeling completely devastated, feeling like everything is over, but my face is completely dry. It's bizarre, honestly, because logically, looking at the data of how I'm feeling, the output should definitely be tears. But the system is just... it's completely locked up. So, no, the tears just aren't there.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, how has your sleep been?\n\nPatient: Sleep? God, my sleep is just... it's a complete disaster. It's completely non-functional. You know how a bridge is supposed to have a period of low stress, low traffic, to just exist without bearing maximum load? Sleep is supposed to be that for the brain, right? But I'm not getting that. At all. \n\nLately, it's like my mind is a turbine that someone left running on high, but there's no water flowing through it, just grinding metal. I'll get into bed, usually around 10:30, because that's always been my schedule, right? I'm very regimented usually. But I'll just lie there. I'll lie awake staring at the ceiling, and the loop starts. It's this endless, agonizing loop where I'm replaying everything. I'll think about the bills—the mortgage on the house that I'm now paying alone, the utilities, the credit card I had to put some of the legal fees on. I start doing the math in my head, over and over, projecting out months in advance, and the numbers never work out. \n\nAnd if it's not the bills, it's work. I'll obsess over a site plan I reviewed three weeks ago. I'll suddenly convince myself I missed a crucial load-bearing calculation on that commercial project downtown. I'll try to visually reconstruct the blueprints in my head in the dark, terrified that I've approved something that's going to collapse, which is ironic given how I feel internally. This will go on for hours. I'll look at the clock and it's 1:00 AM, then 2:30 AM, then 3:45 AM. \n\nAnd then, if I do finally manage to drift off, it's not real sleep. It's this thin, fragile layer of unconsciousness that shatters at the slightest thing. I'll wake up before dawn, like 4:30 or 5:00 in the morning, and my heart is just pounding. The anxiety is instantly there, sitting on my chest like a concrete block. And I feel completely unrefreshed. I feel like I never actually slept at all, like I just closed my eyes and continued worrying on a subconscious level. I drag myself out of bed feeling heavier and more exhausted than when I got in it the night before. It's just a completely unsustainable cycle of fatigue.\n\nDoctor: Over the past month, has your appetite changed?\n\nPatient: I... I really haven't noticed much of a change, to be honest. I mean, my appetite is just sort of... there. It's the same as it usually is, I suppose. \n\nYou know, it's funny, because you hear about people who get so stressed out or depressed and they just stop eating entirely, right? Like their stomach just shrinks up into a little knot and they can't even look at food without feeling nauseous. Or on the flip side, you hear about people who just eat their feelings—they sit on the couch with a pint of ice cream or a whole pizza and just consume until they're numb. But for me, it hasn't really been either of those extremes. It's just... neutral.\n\nI still eat my normal meals. I'll get up, and even though I'm completely exhausted from that terrible sleep cycle I was just describing, I'll still go to the kitchen and make my usual breakfast. It's always oatmeal with a handful of walnuts and half a banana. I've eaten that same breakfast for, I don't know, six or seven years now. It's a very efficient, structurally sound way to start the day, nutritionally speaking. And I still eat it. I don't necessarily savor it or get any joy out of it right now, but I eat it because it's fuel. It's just a mechanical process. \n\nAnd lunch is the same. I usually pack a turkey sandwich on whole wheat or a salad with some grilled chicken. Even with everything feeling like it's crumbling around me, I still go through the motions of putting the food in my mouth and chewing and swallowing. Dinner is maybe a little harder to muster the energy to actually cook—sometimes I'll just heat up a can of soup instead of making a full meal—but I'm still eating a normal amount of food. My stomach still growls around noon, and I still respond to that biological cue. \n\nSo, no, the appetite itself hasn't really changed. The *experience* of eating is definitely completely devoid of pleasure, much like everything else right now, but the actual volume of food and the desire to consume it to keep the machine running... that's all stayed pretty consistent. I haven't lost weight or gained weight or anything like that. It's just one of the few systems that seems to still be operating on its baseline parameters, even if the rest of the structure is failing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Slower? Um... no, I wouldn't say that. I mean, it's an interesting question, because logically, you would assume that with the sheer weight of everything I've been describing—that absolute structural load pressing down on my chest—that my physical mechanics would be compromised. Like a machine operating under way too much friction, right? You'd expect the gears to just grind down and move at half speed. But physically, my velocity is pretty much the exact same. 

I still walk from my car to the office at my usual pace. I've always been a fairly brisk walker, actually. It's an efficiency thing for me, calculating the optimal route and just executing it to get from point A to point B. And even now, despite being so incredibly drained from that terrible sleep cycle, I still maintain that same stride. It's almost like my body is just running on pure muscle memory, this pre-programmed kinetic sequence that just fires off regardless of the internal system failure. I don't shuffle, I don't drag my feet. 

And as for speaking... well, I'm sure you can tell from sitting here with me, I haven't exactly lost my words. I'm not pausing for long periods or dragging my sentences out or struggling to articulate. If anything, when I start trying to explain the site plans or the mortgage or just the general collapse of my daily operations, the words come out just as fast as they ever did. Maybe even a little more urgently, because of that anxious loop I mentioned before. 

So, no, there's no slowness in the actual output. I'm not moving through molasses, which I know is how some people describe feeling when they're going through a hard time. The kinematics of my movement and speech are completely standard. It just... it takes a monumental amount of internal effort to *initiate* the movement in the first place, if that makes sense. Like, getting the engine to turn over takes all my strength, but once it's running, it runs at the normal RPM. So the speed itself hasn't changed at all.
